People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.

But this time I came down on someone harder than I should have.\n\nThat’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people. It bothered me enough that I figured I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with your temper before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. It bothered me enough that I figured I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with your temper before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. When you’re running a company, you learn to check things twice, then someone still manages to miss a supplier email or file something under the wrong folder—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nRepeating actions… no, I haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It just cuts in.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or looking at numbers—spreadsheets, margins, the sort of thing I’ve looked at for years—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are back in my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to watch.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to keep talking about whatever we were talking about, delivery times or costs or some boring line item, but part of me is not there for a minute. It’s annoying. More than annoying, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… I have, I suppose.

I haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. I’ll make some excuse about being tied up with calls or needing someone else to check on something. Yes… yes, more than I like to admit.

If a door shuts hard, or that printer behind me suddenly kicks on, I jump like something’s gone off again. It’s ridiculous—well, it feels ridiculous, anyway. We’ve had that printer for years, temperamental thing, always chewing paper when we need something signed, and normally I’d just swear at it and carry on.

Now my whole body reacts before I can think. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.

There’s this mortgage folder on my desk, and the safety reports too, and I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, then after the next call… and then they just sit there.\n\nThe minute I reach for them, my stomach drops. Like I’m back near all of it again. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.

It’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking down at numbers—same sort of figures I’ve looked at my whole working life—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right back in my head. Like a bad film starting up without me pressing play.

And then, sorry, I know I’m drifting, but numbers used to calm me down, in a way. You know where you are with them—columns, totals, margins. My father used to say if the numbers don’t lie, people will do it for them. But now I can be staring at a page and I’m not really seeing it, because that scene has jumped in again. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.

About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.

It’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. I’ve had board issues drag on for months and somehow those felt more manageable than this. But yes… these thoughts, and that numb sort of feeling, they started right after it happened and they haven’t really let up since.

But this time I came down on someone harder than I should have.\n\nThat’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people. It bothered me enough that I figured I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with your temper before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. When you’re running a company, you learn to check things twice, then someone still manages to miss a supplier email or file something under the wrong folder—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nRepeating actions… no, I haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It just cuts in.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or looking at numbers—spreadsheets, margins, the sort of thing I’ve looked at for years—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are back in my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to watch.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to keep talking about whatever we were talking about, delivery times or costs or some boring line item, but part of me is not there for a minute. It’s annoying. More than annoying, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that are not related to work or daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not really about work, even when it happens during work.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of a call, or staring at numbers—profit margins, invoices, all that usual stuff—and then suddenly the blast comes back. That fire by the loading area. It just jumps into my head, like some awful film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nAnd then I’m still supposed to be talking about delivery costs or whatever, but my mind’s gone somewhere else. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of numbness?\n\nPatient: About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.\n\nIt’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. Yes… I’ve been avoiding the new building. I keep finding reasons not to go over there, even when I probably should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because I lost my temper on a board call. It was over something small, a question about the facility budget, and I just snapped. I mean, I deal with budgets all day, people ask questions, that’s normal—heck, half my job is answering the same question three different ways. But this time I came down on someone harder than I should have.\n\nThat’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people. It bothered me enough that I figured I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with your temper before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. When you’re running a company, you learn to check things twice, then someone still manages to miss a supplier email or file something under the wrong folder—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nRepeating actions… no, I haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It just cuts in.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or looking at numbers—spreadsheets, margins, the sort of thing I’ve looked at for years—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are back in my head. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to watch.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to keep talking about whatever we were talking about, delivery times or costs or some boring line item, but part of me is not there for a minute. It’s annoying. More than annoying, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that are not related to work or daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not really about work, even when it happens during work.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of a call, or staring at numbers—profit margins, invoices, all that usual stuff—and then suddenly the blast comes back. That fire by the loading area. It just jumps into my head, like some awful film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nAnd then I’m still supposed to be talking about delivery costs or whatever, but my mind’s gone somewhere else. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. I’ll make some excuse about being tied up with calls or needing someone else to check on something. And the stupid thing is, it’s not only the place. There’s this mortgage folder on my desk, and the safety reports too, and I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, then after the next call… and then they just sit there.\n\nThe minute I reach for them, my stomach drops. Like I’m back near all of it again. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were reliving the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking down at numbers—same sort of figures I’ve looked at my whole working life—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right back in my head. Like a bad film starting up without me pressing play.\n\nAnd then, sorry, I know I’m drifting, but numbers used to calm me down, in a way. You know where you are with them—columns, totals, margins. My father used to say if the numbers don’t lie, people will do it for them. But now I can be staring at a page and I’m not really seeing it, because that scene has jumped in again. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of numbness?\n\nPatient: About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.\n\nIt’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. I’ve had board issues drag on for months and somehow those felt more manageable than this. But yes… these thoughts, and that numb sort of feeling, they started right after it happened and they haven’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the blast or the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve been avoiding the new building. Yes… yes, I think it has. I’m still working, technically. I mean, I’m taking calls, reading things, showing up where I’m meant to show up—well, mostly.

I mean, I deal with budgets all day, people ask questions, that’s normal—heck, half my job is answering the same question three different ways. But this time I came down on someone harder than I should have.\n\nThat’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people. It bothered me enough that I figured I should talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you had problems with your temper before this incident?\n\nPatient: Not really like this, no. I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. When you’re running a company, you learn to check things twice, then someone still manages to miss a supplier email or file something under the wrong folder—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nRepeating actions… no, I haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It just cuts in.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or looking at numbers—spreadsheets, margins, the sort of thing I’ve looked at for years—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are back in my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to watch.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to keep talking about whatever we were talking about, delivery times or costs or some boring line item, but part of me is not there for a minute. It’s annoying. More than annoying, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that are not related to work or daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not really about work, even when it happens during work.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of a call, or staring at numbers—profit margins, invoices, all that usual stuff—and then suddenly the blast comes back. That fire by the loading area. It just jumps into my head, like some awful film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nAnd then I’m still supposed to be talking about delivery costs or whatever, but my mind’s gone somewhere else. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. I’ll make some excuse about being tied up with calls or needing someone else to check on something. And the stupid thing is, it’s not only the place. There’s this mortgage folder on my desk, and the safety reports too, and I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, then after the next call… and then they just sit there.\n\nThe minute I reach for them, my stomach drops. Like I’m back near all of it again. And I know paperwork is just paperwork—I’ve dealt with worse piles of it for years, believe me—but with those, I just… I don’t want to touch them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected events?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, more than I like to admit.\n\nIf a door shuts hard, or that printer behind me suddenly kicks on, I jump like something’s gone off again. It’s ridiculous—well, it feels ridiculous, anyway. We’ve had that printer for years, temperamental thing, always chewing paper when we need something signed, and normally I’d just swear at it and carry on.\n\nNow my whole body reacts before I can think. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were reliving the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking down at numbers—same sort of figures I’ve looked at my whole working life—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right back in my head. Like a bad film starting up without me pressing play.\n\nAnd then, sorry, I know I’m drifting, but numbers used to calm me down, in a way. You know where you are with them—columns, totals, margins. My father used to say if the numbers don’t lie, people will do it for them. But now I can be staring at a page and I’m not really seeing it, because that scene has jumped in again. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of numbness?\n\nPatient: About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.\n\nIt’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. I’ve had board issues drag on for months and somehow those felt more manageable than this. But yes… these thoughts, and that numb sort of feeling, they started right after it happened and they haven’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the blast or the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve been avoiding the new building. I keep finding reasons not to go over there, even when I probably should.\n\nAnd it’s silly, because paperwork’s usually nothing to me—folders, reports, that sort of thing. I’ve spent half my life with files stacked on desks, and my wife used to say I’d know if one paperclip was out of place. But the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, and I sort of… put my hand back and decide it can wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships or work performance since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has. Things I would normally just handle, I’m pushing to someone else or asking them to make the call first. And I’ve always been the one people come to for a clear answer. That’s been my job for years.\n\nThere was a board call where I reacted badly—more than I should have. I keep thinking about that. Not the whole call, just the moment where I realized, oh, I’m not judging this properly. I’m not leading the way I usually would. So yes, it’s starting to affect work, at least. Relationships… I don’t know, maybe indirectly, but work is where I’ve really noticed it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were reliving the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. But the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, and I sort of… put my hand back and decide it can wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships or work performance since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has. I don’t know that I’d say I’m fully back there, like standing in it again. But it does jump at me.

I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking at numbers—ordinary figures, columns, the kind of thing I’ve looked at my whole life—and suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right there in my head. Like a bad film starting up when I didn’t press play.

And then I lose the thread of what I was doing. Which is irritating, because numbers are supposed to behave themselves. They’re not supposed to drag you off somewhere else.

I mean, I’ve had moments—everybody in a boardroom gets firm sometimes, especially when numbers are tight and people want clean answers to messy problems. You know, facilities budgets are always like that, one repair turns into three, and then someone asks why the line item moved…\n\nBut this was different. I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. I mean, I run a company, so not everybody’s going to like every decision I make—budgets, staffing, facilities, all that stuff can make people sore. You tell someone no on a repair request and suddenly you’re the bad guy, you know?\n\nBut no, that’s not why I came in. I came in because I snapped on that board call over a small question about the facility budget, and that’s just not how I run my company or how I treat people. It bothered me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your behavior or mood lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose so. Not in some dramatic way, but things feel kind of… flat. Like I’ll sit down with a chess problem, which usually settles my head, or I’ll look over my hiking notes—I've got these old notebooks from trips, mileage, weather, little things most people wouldn’t care about—and normally that calms me down.\n\nOr dinner with my wife, even. That should feel good. It’s familiar, you know? But lately I know I’m supposed to be enjoying it, and I just… can’t quite get there. It’s like the feeling doesn’t land.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt emotionally numb lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… numb is probably close. Not like I don’t care about anything, exactly. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. Or we’ll be having dinner together, something normal and pleasant, and I know I should be enjoying it. I can see that I should. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. I mean, I’ve always had routines—running a company will do that to you. If you don’t keep track of the small things, suddenly the big things are on fire. Facilities, invoices, who approved what… it all gets tangled.\n\nAnd I’ve always liked details, like with my hiking notes. I’ll write down mileage and weather and odd little things, but that’s not new. That’s just how I am. I don’t think I’ve noticed some new thing where I’m stuck on routines or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain thoughts or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, maybe. I mean, I’ll go back over things in my head sometimes, but I’ve always done that with work. When you’re running a company, you learn to check things twice, then someone still manages to miss a supplier email or file something under the wrong folder—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nRepeating actions… no, I haven’t noticed myself doing anything like that. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It just cuts in.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or looking at numbers—spreadsheets, margins, the sort of thing I’ve looked at for years—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are back in my head. Like a bad film I didn’t ask to watch.\n\nAnd then I’m trying to keep talking about whatever we were talking about, delivery times or costs or some boring line item, but part of me is not there for a minute. It’s annoying. More than annoying, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having intrusive thoughts that are not related to work or daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not really about work, even when it happens during work.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of a call, or staring at numbers—profit margins, invoices, all that usual stuff—and then suddenly the blast comes back. That fire by the loading area. It just jumps into my head, like some awful film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nAnd then I’m still supposed to be talking about delivery costs or whatever, but my mind’s gone somewhere else. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. I’ll make some excuse about being tied up with calls or needing someone else to check on something. And the stupid thing is, it’s not only the place. There’s this mortgage folder on my desk, and the safety reports too, and I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, then after the next call… and then they just sit there.\n\nThe minute I reach for them, my stomach drops. Like I’m back near all of it again. And I know paperwork is just paperwork—I’ve dealt with worse piles of it for years, believe me—but with those, I just… I don’t want to touch them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected events?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, more than I like to admit.\n\nIf a door shuts hard, or that printer behind me suddenly kicks on, I jump like something’s gone off again. It’s ridiculous—well, it feels ridiculous, anyway. We’ve had that printer for years, temperamental thing, always chewing paper when we need something signed, and normally I’d just swear at it and carry on.\n\nNow my whole body reacts before I can think. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were reliving the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking down at numbers—same sort of figures I’ve looked at my whole working life—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right back in my head. Like a bad film starting up without me pressing play.\n\nAnd then, sorry, I know I’m drifting, but numbers used to calm me down, in a way. You know where you are with them—columns, totals, margins. My father used to say if the numbers don’t lie, people will do it for them. But now I can be staring at a page and I’m not really seeing it, because that scene has jumped in again. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of numbness?\n\nPatient: About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.\n\nIt’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. I’ve had board issues drag on for months and somehow those felt more manageable than this. But yes… these thoughts, and that numb sort of feeling, they started right after it happened and they haven’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the blast or the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve been avoiding the new building. I keep finding reasons not to go over there, even when I probably should.\n\nAnd it’s silly, because paperwork’s usually nothing to me—folders, reports, that sort of thing. I’ve spent half my life with files stacked on desks, and my wife used to say I’d know if one paperclip was out of place. But the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, and I sort of… put my hand back and decide it can wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships or work performance since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has. I’m still working, technically. I mean, I’m taking calls, reading things, showing up where I’m meant to show up—well, mostly.\n\nBut I’ve been putting off decisions about the facility. Things I would normally just handle, I’m pushing to someone else or asking them to make the call first. And I’ve always been the one people come to for a clear answer. That’s been my job for years.\n\nThere was a board call where I reacted badly—more than I should have. I keep thinking about that. Not the whole call, just the moment where I realized, oh, I’m not judging this properly. I’m not leading the way I usually would. So yes, it’s starting to affect work, at least. Relationships… I don’t know, maybe indirectly, but work is where I’ve really noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve never been a perfect sleeper—when you run a company, there’s always some number or contract sitting in the back of your head, like a kettle left on low.\n\nSince this happened… I don’t know. I’ve gone to bed, I’ve gotten up. Some nights feel longer than they should, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s really “trouble sleeping” or just me being old and wound up. I haven’t really kept track.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were actually back at the scene of the blast or the fire by the loading area, reliving it as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m fully back there, like standing in it again. But it does jump at me.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking at numbers—ordinary figures, columns, the kind of thing I’ve looked at my whole life—and suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right there in my head. Like a bad film starting up when I didn’t press play.\n\nAnd then I lose the thread of what I was doing. Which is irritating, because numbers are supposed to behave themselves. They’re not supposed to drag you off somewhere else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Mostly it’s those things, yes—the new building, and the papers tied to it. The mortgage folder is sitting there, and the safety reports too. I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, or after one more call, and then somehow it’s the end of the day.

I snapped over a small question on that board call, about the facility budget, and I came down harder than I should have. That’s not how I run my company, and it’s not how I treat people.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you have a difficult personality or that you're hard to get along with?\n\nPatient: I don’t think people would usually say I’m hard to get along with. It’s more that the usual things don’t quite reach me.\n\nI’ll sit with a chess problem, and normally I can lose myself in that for a bit—same with my hiking notes, those old mileage and weather notes I keep, which my wife thinks are too detailed. But it just feels flat right now, like I can’t quite get to the feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself spending a lot of time thinking about specific details or routines?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot of time,” no. Not counting normal habits, I guess. I’m particular about some things, but I don’t think there’s some new loop I’m stuck in.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that you can't stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, not like I’m choosing to sit there and think about it. It’s not really about work, even when it happens during work.\n\nLike I’ll be in the middle of a call, or staring at numbers—profit margins, invoices, all that usual stuff—and then suddenly the blast comes back. That fire by the loading area. It just jumps into my head, like some awful film I didn’t ask to see.\n\nAnd then I’m still supposed to be talking about delivery costs or whatever, but my mind’s gone somewhere else. It’s not like I’m thinking, “Oh, let me remember that now.” It just barges in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have, I suppose.\n\nI haven’t been going near the new building if I can help it. I’ll make some excuse about being tied up with calls or needing someone else to check on something. And the stupid thing is, it’s not only the place. Like I’m back near all of it again. And I know paperwork is just paperwork—I’ve dealt with worse piles of it for years, believe me—but with those, I just… I don’t want to touch them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected events?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, more than I like to admit.\n\nIf a door shuts hard, or that printer behind me suddenly kicks on, I jump like something’s gone off again. It’s ridiculous—well, it feels ridiculous, anyway. We’ve had that printer for years, temperamental thing, always chewing paper when we need something signed, and normally I’d just swear at it and carry on.\n\nNow my whole body reacts before I can think. My hands shake a bit, and it takes me a few minutes to get them steady again. Then I’m sitting there pretending I meant to move or reach for something, you know.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were reliving the blast or the fire by the loading area?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s the closest way to say it, I think.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there and choose to think about it. I’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking down at numbers—same sort of figures I’ve looked at my whole working life—and then suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right back in my head. I keep finding reasons not to go over there, even when I probably should.\n\nAnd it’s silly, because paperwork’s usually nothing to me—folders, reports, that sort of thing. Like a bad film starting up without me pressing play.\n\nAnd then, sorry, I know I’m drifting, but numbers used to calm me down, in a way. You know where you are with them—columns, totals, margins. My father used to say if the numbers don’t lie, people will do it for them. But now I can be staring at a page and I’m not really seeing it, because that scene has jumped in again. It feels like I’m there for a moment, or too close to it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these intrusive thoughts and feelings of numbness?\n\nPatient: About six days now, I think. Since the explosion and the fire during that walkthrough.\n\nIt’s strange, because six days doesn’t sound long when you say it out loud—half a working week, really, depending how you count it. I’ve had board issues drag on for months and somehow those felt more manageable than this. But yes… these thoughts, and that numb sort of feeling, they started right after it happened and they haven’t really let up since.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific activities or situations that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the blast or the fire?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve been avoiding the new building. I keep finding reasons not to go over there, even when I probably should.\n\nAnd it’s silly, because paperwork’s usually nothing to me—folders, reports, that sort of thing. I’ve spent half my life with files stacked on desks, and my wife used to say I’d know if one paperclip was out of place. But the mortgage folder and the safety reports are just sitting there unopened. The minute I reach for them, my stomach drops, and I sort of… put my hand back and decide it can wait.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your relationships or work performance since the incident?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has. I’m still working, technically. I mean, I’m taking calls, reading things, showing up where I’m meant to show up—well, mostly.\n\nBut I’ve been putting off decisions about the facility. Things I would normally just handle, I’m pushing to someone else or asking them to make the call first. And I’ve always been the one people come to for a clear answer. That’s been my job for years.\n\nThere was a board call where I reacted badly—more than I should have. I keep thinking about that. Not the whole call, just the moment where I realized, oh, I’m not judging this properly. I’m not leading the way I usually would. So yes, it’s starting to affect work, at least. Relationships… I don’t know, maybe indirectly, but work is where I’ve really noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the incident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I’ve never been a perfect sleeper—when you run a company, there’s always some number or contract sitting in the back of your head, like a kettle left on low.\n\nSince this happened… I don’t know. I’ve gone to bed, I’ve gotten up. Some nights feel longer than they should, but I couldn’t tell you if that’s really “trouble sleeping” or just me being old and wound up. I haven’t really kept track.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt like you were actually back at the scene of the blast or the fire by the loading area, reliving it as if it were happening again?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’m fully back there, like standing in it again. But it does jump at me.\n\nI’ll be in the middle of a call, or just looking at numbers—ordinary figures, columns, the kind of thing I’ve looked at my whole life—and suddenly the blast and that fire by the loading area are right there in my head. Like a bad film starting up when I didn’t press play.\n\nAnd then I lose the thread of what I was doing. Which is irritating, because numbers are supposed to behave themselves. They’re not supposed to drag you off somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other activities or situations, besides the new building and certain paperwork, that you've been avoiding because they remind you of the blast or the fire?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s those things, yes—the new building, and the papers tied to it. The mortgage folder is sitting there, and the safety reports too. I keep telling myself I’ll open them after lunch, or after one more call, and then somehow it’s the end of the day.\n\nIt’s ridiculous, really. I’ve signed stacks of paperwork thicker than a phone book—back when phone books were a thing, anyway. But now the minute I reach for that folder, my stomach just drops, and I find some other task to do. So… no, not many other situations I can name. It’s mainly that building and those documents."
